As the wind shifts over seasons and years , the sea surface temperatures ( SST ) naturally warm and cool from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> El Ni ? o is when we have mean SST + 0.5 degrees Celsius above the climate average over a three-month period . <p> La Ni ? a is when we have mean SST -0.5 degrees Celsius below the climate average over a three-month period . <h> What impacts does it have on weather ? <h> Weather <p> The biggest impacts around Chicago happen during the winter months , when moisture and storm patterns are more easily steered by dynamics in the jet stream . <p> In short , it usually means warmer and drier winters for Chicago . <p> It also could lead to drought conditions for parts of the Midwest and the Pacific Northwest , while heavy rain and flooding are more likely from southern California to the Gulf Coast . <p> The stronger the ENSO event is , the more extreme the weather impacts could be . That could mean an intense drought for some , and intense flooding for others . <h> What is a " super El Ni ? o " ? <p> It 's not a technical term . Ultimately , it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , meaning temperatures are far above average . <p> A weak El Ni ? o is considered between 0.5 degrees and 0.9 degrees Celsius . <p> A moderate El Ni ? o is 1 degree to 1.4 degrees Celsius . <p> A strong , or " super , " El Ni ? o is equal to or greater than 1.5 degrees Celsius . <p> The last strong El Ni ? o we had was 2015-2016 when the SST was +2.4 ? C in the November , December , January three-month average . <p> That winter finished with about 16 inches of snow and temperatures +4.2 ? from average . <h> How could climate change effect El Ni ? o ? <p> Scientists estimate the ocean is absorbing and storing about 90% of the excess heat from global warming . As the world and oceans continue to warm , stronger El Ni ? o events are expected . <p> The ENSO forecast : <p> There 's a 62% chance of El Ni ? o emerging by the June-July-August time period , and it 's expected to strengthen going into winter . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ strong El Ni ? o , so we 'll see how they evolve in the coming months . 
@@136130698 <p> Now that the Federal Court of Appeal has upheld the Canadian government 's authority to ban single-use plastic items , it is time for Ottawa to take the next step and close the door on misleading claims that so-called " compostable " products and packaging belong in home or backyard composters . <p> Composting is a vital way to improve soil health , support food security and fight climate change , but its success depends on public trust in composting systems . <p> However , actions by some manufacturers , packaging suppliers and certification bodies to introduce " home compostable " claims in Canada -- claims that do not necessarily hold up in real-world conditions -- threaten to undermine public trust and participation in the entire organics recycling system . <p> Not only must companies and their industry partners refrain from these claims , but the federal government must immediately intervene to ban the labelling of products or packaging as " home " or " backyard " compostable . <p> While composting is not new -- Canadians have been using backyard composters to turn food scraps and yard waste @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ increasingly recognized as a crucial solution for reducing the amount of organic waste in landfill sites . <p> The problem is not all products and packaging labelled compostable truly are , and there are no enforceable rules in Canada preventing these claims . <p> At the Compost Council of Canada , we are a national leader in spearheading certification standards for industrial composting , but we have consistently opposed certification as inappropriate for backyard composting due to our climate and composting realities , and federal regulatory direction . <p> Current home-compostable certification programs are not designed for Canadian real-world conditions . Current international standards are based on laboratory testing that uses ambient temperatures of a minimum of 20 C for up to 180 consecutive days . <p> This does not match weather conditions anywhere in Canada , where outdoor temperatures simply do not get high enough for long enough to fully break down items claiming to be backyard compostable . <p> While these items might decompose in a few weeks or months in an industrial composter -- where temperature , moisture and aeration are better controlled -- they often remain @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ even years . <p> And unlike lab testing , in the real world these items are unlikely to be in pristine condition when put in backyard compost bins . Instead , they might contain traces of food waste -- crumbs or meat or dairy scraps -- not meant for backyard composting , since home systems do n't usually reach high enough temperatures and could attract unwanted critters . <p> Introducing home compostable claims that do not reflect real-world conditions jeopardizes decades of progress . <p> There is also a risk that consumers might assume all items of a certain type -- like plates or cups -- are backyard compostable if one brand is labelled that way , even if the others are not . <p> Introducing home compostable claims that do not reflect real-world conditions jeopardizes decades of progress in promoting composting as a meaningful way to process organic materials and create quality compost . <p> It also risks eroding consumer confidence in composting overall -- hindering participation and damaging the reputation of both municipal-industrial and home composting programs . <p> Composting realities in Canada also call into question whether @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Statistics Canada reports a 51 per cent decline in backyard composting across Canada for kitchen waste and a 38 per cent decline for yard waste since 2009 . Quebec has seen declines of 75 per cent ( kitchen waste ) and 49 per cent ( yard waste ) over the same period . <p> The federal government has also previously indicated it plans to prohibit labelling that uses home or backyard compostable claims . <p> The problem is that it put draft regulations on hold pending the Federal Court ruling . This delay has left a gap that undermines the integrity of compostable packaging and product claims . <p> It is imperative that the government follow through on its 2023 initiative to fix weak or non-existent regulatory oversight on false labelling claims to prevent home or backyard compostable claims . <p> Composting is a hands-on way for people to take part in the full cycle of organics recycling , but if misleading backyard compostable claims shatter public trust and participation , Canada 's entire organics recycling system is at risk . <p> Industry partners must step away from making home @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ government must implement regulations to ensure that they do . <p> There is too much at stake . <p> Susan Antler is the executive director for the Compost Council of Canada , the national non-profit organization dedicated to the advancement of organics recycling and soil health.Benoit Lamarche is the chair of the Quebec chapter . @@136140991 <p> Mindful eating : How to slow down , enjoy your meal and appreciate the food : Life KitWhen we scarf down our food , we do n't get a chance to properly digest or appreciate it . Lilian Cheung , a mindful eating lecturer at Harvard , shares techniques on how to slow down the pace . <p> This story was originally published on Sept. 9 , 2023 , and has been updated . <p> You ever eat so fast that you get hiccups from just like inhaling the meal ? Or bite your cheek or tongue because you mistook it for food ? <p> Yeah , I 've done it . <p> To slow down the pace , you 'll need to practice mindful eating . That means using your senses to actually enjoy the food , taking the time to express gratitude for your meal , and making " choices that are satisfying and nourishing to the body , " says Lilian Cheung , lecturer and director of Mindfulness Research and Practice in the Department of Nutrition at Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ digestion , it " allows us to become much more aware of the food we have , how we get it and what it takes to be able to have that , " she says . <h> How to eat more mindfully <p> Remove all distractions while you eat -- including your cell phone . They can interfere with your ability to enjoy your food and notice when you are full . " Allocate time to eat and only eat , " says Cheung . " Make sure your cell phone is face down and you 're not going to be responding to any messages that come through . " <p> See if you can stretch your mealtime to 20 minutes . Very often we find ourselves eating while doing something else , says Cheung , and that can make us eat faster than we normally would . When you sit down to eat , spend about 20 minutes doing so . " It takes about that time for your body to get the signal to the brain that you are full , " she adds . <p> Buy time @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about your food . You might wonder how to spend 20 whole minutes eating a sandwich . Cheung says one way to slow down is to engage your senses and think through all the details about your meal . " Ask yourself : what 's on my plate ? How hungry am I today ? Is it too salty ? " she says . Notice the smell , the texture and whatever other senses that arise as you eat . <p> Sponsor Message <p> You might also think about the effort it took to get the food on the table . If you 're eating potato chips , for example , you might " thank the universe for the right climate to have that potato , the manpower engaged in making it available , the transportation to get the chips to the supermarket , " she says . <p> Actually chew . If you 're inhaling your food you 're probably not chewing it . And chewing is an important part of digestion , says Cheung . It helps " break up the foods so it 's easier for absorption @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ your mouth , acknowledge what you 're eating and " chew , chew , chew , " she adds . <p> Use mealtime as a moment for reflection . Cheung shares an invocation she learned from the late Buddhist monk Th ? ch Nh ? t Hanh called " The Five Contemplations , " outlined in his book Savor : Mindful Eating , Mindful Life . She says that reciting these lines before meals have helped her practice mindful eating . <p> Reciting " The Five Contemplations " for mindful eating by the late Buddhist monk Th ? ch Nh ? t Hanh before a meal can help you eat more slowly and deliberately , says Lilian Cheung , a lecturer and the director of Mindfulness Research and Practice in the Department of Nutrition at Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health . @@136191381 <p> On June 18 , 1965 , a photo on the front page of the New York Times forever changed the trajectory of my life . I was an angsty 14-year-old , sprawled on the white shag carpet of my parents ' Upper East Side apartment , mesmerized by a photo taken by Horst Faas in a place called Vietnam . And where on Earth was Vietnam ? <p> That photo turned the dial of my life 's direction 180 degrees and sent me off into the world to find out . <p> Today , that helmet is displayed at the National Museum of American History . @@136241198 <h> OPINION EDITORIAL : Reality of the now <h> All hands on deck , in fuel fight <p> 14 hours , 49 minutes ago <p> Let us read it for you . Listen now . Your browser does not support the audio element . <p> It appears that Mexico is finally open to an idea that changed Arkansas and the rest of the energy world more than a decade ago , when the Barnett Shale natural gas play began operations in Texas , soon followed by Arkansas ' Fayetteville Shale . <p> The breakthrough technology unlocked loads of natural gas into the marketplace and sent the price for natural gas through the floor . When the technology was slightly modified to produce oil , America became the No. 1 producer of oil in the world again . This has been good news for the country . <p> In order to access this unconventional gas , the process of hydraulic fracturing , or fracking , must be used . It 's considered unconventional because the stuff we 're trying to get at is stuck inside shale--until that shale is fractured , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ market . <p> However , Mexican President , Claudia Sheinbaum is a scientist and climate expert and understands the negative connotation associated with fracking . <p> She does n't like the term and has a problem with the fact that her country is too dependent on foreign sources of energy that may supersede environmental concerns . So she 's calling it " sustainable fracking " ( to the chagrin of the environmental community ) . <p> " All the gas we import comes from a type of extraction that has environmental impacts " and is " about 110 yards from the Mexican border , " she said . Mexico is the single largest buyer of U.S. natural gas , according to The Associated Press . <p> " Is more gas needed ? Yes . Can all gas be replaced ? Hardly , " she added . <p> Despite her concerns about using the word " fracking , " she has firmly supported the state-owned oil company Petroleos Mexicanos . She 's defended her dual support for fossil and renewable energy sources because of the recognition that fossil fuels will be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ price spike exposure like we see today . <p> When it comes to energy issues , she 's the type of elected leader America needs . One who recognizes that calling climate change a hoax is based in politics and not science , but also understands the modern world in which we live , and that it requires oil and gas . <p> America could use leaders who understand these things . While it would be a positive step to wean ourselves from many fuel sources while promoting less-polluting renewable fuels , that 's not going to happen overnight . <p> It should n't be a fight over what 's the best fuel we can produce , but an all-hands effort to figure out how renewable and fossil energy sources can peacefully co-exist . This would blend , helpfully , what we want for the future and reality of the now .

Over a ten-day period , four astronauts flew to the Moon and back , becoming the first people to visit Earth 's satellite in more than half a century , and travelling farther than anyone has gone before . <p> Judging by the breadth and depth of international media coverage , many have been inspired by the voyage . Meanwhile , for many , a return to the Moon seems an expensive distraction from longer-term problems on Earth , such as climate change and environmental degradation . <p> But there is much to celebrate . The Artemis II mission showed the world what curiosity and collaboration can achieve . It was not , strictly speaking , a scientific flight , yet it demonstrated some of the best aspects of science . Despite the conflicts and other challenges that humanity is facing on Earth , Artemis II was a global project , driven by a shared spirit of discovery . <p> Although NASA was at the controls , Artemis II was a multinational endeavour . The European Space Agency 's service module provided the power and propulsion needed to keep NASA 's Orion capsule going . The Canadian Space Agency cheered the presence of its astronaut , Jeremy Hansen , on board . Crew member Christina Koch reiterated the message of human connectivity after the astronauts briefly lost contact with Earth as they traversed the far side of the Moon and began their voyage home . " We will always choose each other , " she said . It was a powerful message . <p> Human space flight has always been a polarizing issue , with some in favour and others opposed . Today , robots can accomplish many of the goals of space exploration , making it unnecessary to endanger human lives . <p> Human space flight also has political overtones . Countries that send astronauts into space do so to project both hard and soft power . And the programmes are expensive . According to the NASA inspector-general 's office , the Artemis II launch cost at least US$4 billion . Even as NASA pushes forwards with human space flight , it must also find the funds to support missions led by the scientific community across a range of research disciplines . <p> The Artemis II crew captured this image of a crescent Earth ' setting ' on the Moon.Credit : NASA <p> All in all , the cost of Artemis II , which is a small fraction of US government spending , needs to be assessed alongside the mission 's benefits . @@136275187 <h> Why " Mini " Nuclear Reactors Might Not Fix Our Climate Crisis <p> Small Modular Reactors ( SMRs ) are often presented as a promising innovation in the quest for low-carbon energy , but their potential impact remains a subject of debate . These reactors , capable of generating up to 300 megawatts of electricity per unit , are designed for modular construction and factory production , which could reduce costs and streamline deployment compared to traditional nuclear plants . However , as Just Have a Think explores , SMRs face significant hurdles , including the high costs of advanced fuels like high-assay low-enriched uranium ( HALEU ) and the challenge of scaling production to achieve meaningful economic benefits . These factors raise important questions about whether SMRs can deliver on their promises or risk diverting resources from established renewable energy solutions . <p> In this deep dive , you 'll gain insight into the specific advantages SMRs offer , such as their potential to retrofit retiring coal plants and provide continuous power for hard-to-decarbonize industries . You 'll also explore the critical challenges they face , from nuclear @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ By examining these factors , you 'll better understand the nuanced role SMRs could play in the global energy transition and the trade-offs involved in prioritizing their development alongside other clean energy technologies . <p> The future of SMRs depends on overcoming economic , technical and regulatory hurdles to prove their viability as a strategic tool in global decarbonization efforts . <p> SMRs represent a new generation of nuclear technology , designed to be smaller , more adaptable and easier to construct than traditional nuclear power plants . Unlike conventional reactors , which are often associated with delays and cost overruns due to their size and complexity , SMRs aim to simplify the construction process through modular designs and factory-based production . These reactors are shipped as pre-fabricated components and assembled on-site , reducing both construction timelines and costs . <p> SMRs come in various designs , including light-water reactors , molten salt reactors and high-temperature gas reactors . Each design offers unique features , such as improved safety mechanisms or the ability to operate at higher temperatures . Leading companies like Westinghouse , Rolls-Royce and GE Hitachi are at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ construction or in advanced planning stages . These advancements highlight the growing interest in SMRs as a potential solution to the challenges of traditional nuclear energy . <h> Key Advantages of SMRs <p> Proponents of SMRs highlight several potential benefits that could make them a valuable addition to the global energy mix . These advantages include : <p> Modularity : SMRs are designed for factory production , allowing for faster assembly and reduced construction complexity . This approach could significantly lower costs compared to traditional nuclear plants . <p> Standardization : The mass production of identical units could lead to economies of scale , addressing one of the nuclear industry 's long-standing challenges of high upfront costs . <p> Beyond electricity generation , SMRs have the potential to support a range of industrial applications . These include hydrogen production , high-temperature heat for manufacturing processes and even retrofitting retiring coal plants . By using existing infrastructure and workforce expertise , SMRs could assist a smoother transition for communities currently dependent on fossil fuels , offering a pathway to economic and environmental sustainability . <h> Economic and Technical Challenges <p> Despite @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Critics argue that reducing the size of reactors does not proportionally lower costs . Fixed expenses , such as safety systems , regulatory compliance and operational oversight , remain substantial regardless of reactor size . Achieving meaningful cost reductions through mass production requires high manufacturing volumes , a challenge that the nuclear industry has historically struggled to meet . <p> Another critical issue is the availability of fuel . Many advanced SMR designs rely on high-assay low-enriched uranium ( HALEU ) , a specialized fuel with limited global supply . Currently , much of the HALEU production capacity is concentrated in Russia , raising concerns about supply chain security . Establishing a reliable and diversified HALEU supply chain will require significant investments in new fabrication facilities , regulatory approvals and international collaboration . <h> Environmental and Waste Management Concerns <p> Nuclear waste remains a persistent challenge for SMRs . While some designs claim to produce less waste or recycle spent fuel more efficiently , others may generate new types of waste or even higher volumes . These waste streams will require long-term storage solutions , which are already a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sustainable waste management strategies will be essential to gaining public trust and securing regulatory approval for SMR deployment . <p> Additionally , the environmental impact of mining and processing nuclear fuel can not be overlooked . While SMRs offer low-carbon energy during operation , their full lifecycle emissions and environmental footprint must be carefully evaluated to ensure they align with broader sustainability goals . <h> Strategic Role in Decarbonization <p> SMRs have the potential to play a strategic role in the global energy transition by providing reliable , low-carbon power . Their ability to operate continuously makes them particularly well-suited for industrial applications , such as steelmaking and cement production , which are challenging to decarbonize using intermittent renewable energy sources . Retrofitting existing coal plants with SMRs could also extend the life of valuable infrastructure while significantly reducing emissions . <p> However , deployment timelines remain a significant concern . As of today , only two SMRs are operational worldwide and widespread commercial viability is not expected until the mid-2030s . This delay raises questions about whether SMRs can contribute meaningfully to urgent decarbonization efforts . Meanwhile , renewable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rapidly scaling and becoming more cost-effective , further intensifying the debate over resource allocation . <h> Renewables vs . SMRs : Competing Priorities <p> The debate surrounding SMRs often centers on the allocation of public funding and policy support . Renewable energy technologies , such as solar , wind and battery storage , have already demonstrated their scalability and cost-effectiveness in reducing greenhouse gas emissions . Critics argue that investing in SMRs , a relatively unproven technology , could divert resources from these proven solutions . <p> On the other hand , advocates contend that SMRs could complement renewables by providing firm , low-carbon power to balance the variability of wind and solar energy . This hybrid approach could enhance grid reliability and accelerate the transition to a decarbonized energy system . By filling gaps in renewable energy production , SMRs could help address the challenges of intermittency and ensure a stable energy supply . <h> The Future of SMRs in the Energy Mix <p> The future of SMRs will depend on their ability to overcome economic , technical and regulatory challenges . If they can deliver on promises of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ become a valuable tool in the fight against climate change . Their potential to support industrial decarbonization and complement renewable energy sources positions them as a promising , albeit uncertain , addition to the energy landscape . <p> For now , SMRs remain a technology in development , with much work needed to prove their viability . Whether they emerge as a cornerstone of the global energy transition or a costly diversion will depend on how effectively the industry addresses its challenges . As the world races to meet ambitious climate goals , the clock is ticking for SMRs to demonstrate their value in the evolving energy mix .

@@136284199 <p> 5 ways to electrify your home and transportation : Life KitTo live a more climate friendly life , invest in home appliances and vehicles that run on electricity , not fossil fuels , say sustainability experts . Here are a few energy-saving solutions to consider . <p> Buying appliances and vehicles that run on electricity , not fossil fuels , can help reduce our carbon footprint . Making these upgrades will cost money -- so you will need to plan ahead , says Joel Rosenberg of the nonprofit group Rewiring America . <p> Driving a car , making dinner , heating water and turning on the air conditioner -- our everyday actions emit some of the greenhouse gases that contribute to climate change . <p> But sustainability experts say there are ways we can make these daily tasks more climate friendly . By using home appliances and vehicles that run on electricity , we can help reduce our carbon footprint and leave more fossil fuels in the ground . While these individual moves wo n't reverse climate change , they are smaller steps that each of us can take @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Making these upgrades to your home and lifestyle will cost money -- and you will need to plan ahead , says Joel Rosenberg of the nonprofit group Rewiring America and the author of Electrify Everything in Your Home . But if you have a boiler and radiators , costs can be high because you 'll have to install air ducts . <p> " On the low end you might be able to get away with $5,000 , and on the high end , it might be $50,000 , to get your whole home to run entirely on a heat pump , " says Rosenberg . <p> Sponsor Message <p> " Heat pump " is a technology that comes up a lot when talking about energy efficiency . They are used for heating and cooling for a range of appliances . Essentially , they move hot air around to where you want it through a process similar to what a refrigerator uses . And they require less energy to do the same job . <p> Budget options : There are cheaper ways to cut heating and cooling costs , like using portable heat pumps , which can cost as little as a few hundred dollars . These can be good for an apartment or for just one room in your house . An even simpler solution @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and lower it a few degrees in winter , so your air conditioner or furnace does n't run as often . Or consider a smart thermostat that connects to your phone . These give you more control over when your system runs and some will automatically go into an energy-saving mode when you leave home . <h> Upgrade your water heater <p> If you 're thinking about a heat pump water heater , plan ahead . These heaters are taller so may need more ceiling space . And if your current water heater is powered by gas , you 'll need to get electricity installed . The Washington Post/The Washington Post via Getty Images hide caption <p> toggle caption <p> The Washington Post/The Washington Post via Getty Images <p> Maybe you do n't think about that water heater in your basement or closet , but you should . It accounts for about 18% of a home 's energy use . <p> Consumer Reports ranks water heaters that use heat pump technology as better than gas or traditional electric ones , though they cost more than twice as much . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ electric and gas ones are about $400 . <p> " But if you look across all of the years of ownership , it levels itself out maybe after about two to three years of use , " says Whitehurst . Since they 're two to three times more efficient than most electric water heaters , they can help save money on utility bills . <p> If you 're sold on getting a heat pump water heater , plan ahead . These heaters are taller , so if your water heater is in the basement , you 'll need to check and make sure it 'll fit . They ca n't be in a super cold place because they move heat from the room into the water . They also dehumidify the environment , so you 'll want to think about whether that will be a problem . And if your current water heater is powered by gas , you 'll need to get electricity installed . <p> Sponsor Message <p> Budget option : Cheaper and easier-to-install alternatives are available too . Rosenberg suggests screwing more efficient aerators onto your sink @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ comes with an extra benefit , he says . " When you use hot water , you 're using less of it and therefore less of it needs to be heated to replace it . " <h> Look for dryers with the ' Energy Star ' label <p> Dryers that have the Energy Star label use about 20% less energy than regular dryers . Bloomberg/Bloomberg via Getty Images hide caption <p> toggle caption <p> Bloomberg/Bloomberg via Getty Images <p> For clothes dryers , or just about any appliance , there 's a federal program that 'll help you save energy . It 's called Energy Star . Manufacturers that have made efficient appliances get to use the label -- just look for that and you know you 're getting some of the most climate friendly appliances available . <p> Energy Star clothes dryers use about 20% less energy . If all Americans switched to Energy Star dryers , that would collectively save $1.5 billion in energy costs and avoid about the same amount of pollution as 2 million cars , according to the U.S. Department of Energy . <p> Just @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ clothes dryers available now too . These recycle hot air rather than vent it outside . But clothing can take longer to dry and the appliance can cost twice as much as a conventional dryer . <p> Budget option : The simplest way to save energy and money is to dry clothes the way generations did before us . " Growing up , we had a clothesline outside and it 's one of the options you always forget about , " says Whitehurst . If you ca n't hang a clothesline outside , there are inexpensive drying racks you can use indoors . <p> " The ideal , if you need a car , is to get an electric car , " says Rosenberg . " Every year , more and more cars are being manufactured that fit many needs , from pickup trucks to electric bicycles . " <p> Sponsor Message <p> Electric vehicles are also getting more affordable . A small EV , such as the Nissan Leaf , starts around $30,000 . That 's $10,000 more than a comparable gas car , but the federal government has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ offer subsidies on top of that . <p> Installing a charger at home adds to the cost but some utilities offer incentives , such as cheaper charging during off-peak hours . <p> As with most climate friendly purchases , even baby steps are helpful . If an EV is n't the right choice for you , consider a hybrid car . The gas-and-battery combination improves gas mileage , which saves you money at the pump . Hybrids do cost more regular cars , but Consumer Reports finds the average payback period from gas costs has declined , from eight years to four since 2015 . <p> Budget option : Rosenberg recognizes that most Americans still rely on cars to get around . But if your city has great public transportation , take the bus or subway when you can . It 's cheap and saves energy . <p> Addressing climate change involves a lot of changes across the globe . Mistakes are going to happen . If you 've recently made a big purchase only to learn it was n't the most climate friendly choice , Rosenberg says do n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ What we are strongly in favor of is that you try to commit to not buying any more fossil fuel appliances , " he says . <p> Loading ... <p> This episode of Life Kit was produced by Carly Rubin and edited by Sylvie Douglis . @@136314985 <h> The study will track about 200,000 children born between 2026 and 2029 , along with their families , throughout their lives . <p> Finland is launching a century-long scientific project , " Future Finland , " to map the building blocks of social well-being and happiness over time . The study will track about 200,000 children born between 2026 and 2029 , along with their families , throughout their lives . It will gather data on mental health , social relationships , connection with nature , trust in institutions , and genetics . Methods include questionnaires , biological sampling , and long-term health records . The goal is to show how childhood environment , health , education , and social ties shape quality of life and happiness . <p> Leaders say the project will test whether Finland 's high happiness levels are stable or could change under pressures such as geopolitical shifts , economic crises , migration , and climate change . The century-long perspective is intended to answer questions that can not yet be asked and to guide welfare and health policy in Finland and beyond . <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ strong " laboratory " for this work due to its solid healthcare system , high social trust , excellent public education , and a relatively demographically homogeneous population . Low inequality and a close connection to nature are also cited contributors to its high happiness index . <p> " We believe that the trajectory of human well-being can be changed even within a single generation , " said researcher Mika Salminen . " We can not solve problems whose roots we do not understand , " he added . <p> Finland continues to top global happiness rankings . The country has been recognized as the happiest in the world for eight consecutive years . This status is linked to income , healthcare , social trust , generosity , low corruption , and social systems that reduce stress such as free education and universal healthcare . <p> The national tourism organization is translating those habits into a public invitation through a " Chill Like a Finn " program offering six couples a free one-week trip to Lakeland in June . Applications are open until March 29 via short social-media videos . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ activities common in Finnish leisure : hiking in Kolovesi National Park , swimming in lakes , sauna sessions , reading , napping in hammocks , and relaxed dinners by the fire . It is framed as a way to balance nature , relaxation , and disconnection from modern life -- elements the long-term study will examine as potential drivers of well-being . <h> Contact with nature <p> Contact with nature is a fundamental part of Finnish daily life . Finnish practices built around outdoor access , routine physical activity , and opportunities for solitude and reflection are often cited as contributors to well-being . <h> Followed for a hundred years <p> By structuring the research over 100 years , researchers aim to track how external shocks and policy choices influence life outcomes from birth through old age . The design focuses on how early-life context , education pathways , healthcare access , and the density and quality of social relationships shape later resilience , happiness , and health . <p> Participants will be followed continuously . The study will integrate biological markers with longitudinal assessments of mental health and social @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ life .

@@136372789 <h> A 19-year-old says common sense legislation proposed in the General Assembly would put accountability in place -- before it 's too late . <p> A proposed law in Illinois would require AI developers or large chat providers to " write , implement , comply with , and clearly and conspicuously publish " public safety and child protection plans on their websites . <p> My generation did n't get a grace period . We were the kids doing active-shooter drills in elementary school . We spent a chunk of our high school years staring at Zoom screens during the pandemic . We watched wildfires turn the sky orange while adults debated whether climate change was real . We grew up under a social media ecosystem that was running experiments on our mental health . Now we 're entering a job market that expects us to be grateful for the chance to compete over a shrinking slice of what 's left . <p> The pattern goes like this : A powerful industry emerges , moves fast , promises transformation and asks the public to trust that self-regulation will be enough . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ harms start showing up -- in our lungs , our app feeds and our schools . By the time anyone in Washington , D.C. , actually does something about it , the kids who grew up breathing that air or scrolling those feeds are already carrying the consequences with them . We are living this with fossil fuel , social media and now with AI . <p> Fortunately , there is a common sense bill in Illinois that can proactively protect us before the harms snowball . The Artificial Intelligence Public Safety and Child Protection Transparency Act is n't trying to kill AI or go after the companies building it . The point is simple : Get basic transparency and accountability in place now , while we still can . <p> Commentary bug <h> Commentary <p> California understood this need when it enacted SB 53 in September 2025 , becoming the first state to require standardized safety disclosures from the developers of the most advanced AI systems . New York followed with the RAISE Act , creating its own reporting and governance framework for frontier models . Illinois now has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ decided the public deserves to know what the most powerful AI companies know and are doing about the risks of their own technology . <p> The core idea behind the Illinois bill is one that we already accept in virtually every other industry where the stakes are high . Pharmaceutical companies do n't get to decide for themselves whether their drugs are safe . They 're mandated to publish clinical trial data , submit to independent audits and even report adverse events to an entire federal agency dedicated to exactly that . Airline companies do n't get to investigate crashes in private -- they , too , answer to an agency responsible for that . It 's a clear precedent of oversight : Industries with the capacity to cause large-scale harm need external accountability structures . <p> If we demand this level of accountability from industries that have had decades to prove themselves , it 's hard to see why we would n't expect at least the same from a technology that has been in the public 's hands for less than three years -- with the greatest potential to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ proposed legislation calls for is particularly radical . It simply says that the largest developers operating at the frontier of AI technology should document how their companies are thinking about catastrophic risks , publish a safety plan , report serious incidents to the attorney general and let independent auditors check their work . A company running a chatbot that a million minors can access needs a documented strategy for protecting them . <p> And if none of this is enough to make the case for acting now , then by default , we would be opting to repeat what has not worked for my generation -- waiting while harms proliferate . Wait for another incident that makes the front page of the New York Times , wait for another tech CEO to testify they 'll do better at congressional hearings , wait for the legislation that arrives years too late and applies to a landscape that has already moved on . We have lived through enough of those cycles to know that waiting is a choice , and a bad one . <p> More than anyone else , my generation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are the ones who will raise children in a world where these systems are likely integrated into every institution . <p> It 's reasonable to ask our representatives to pass a bill that requires the most powerful AI companies to be honest about what they know , protect the kids using their products and let someone check their safety work . For a generation that grew up as the test subjects of the " move fast and figure it out later " approach , it might be one of the most reasonable things our legislators can do . <p> Kashyap Rajesh , 19 , grew up in Buffalo Grove and attends Cornell University . He is a member of Design It For Us , a youth-led advocacy organization working to counter Big Tech 's influence in policy . <p> Todd Lyons , who on Thursday resigned from his position as acting director of ICE , also claimed in the letter to the Illinois congressional delegation that no U.S. citizens were arrested by federal immigration officers during Operation Midway Blitz -- despite documented reports . 
@@136401187 <p> The evolutionary edge that fueled great white shark dominance for millions of years could soon become its greatest downfall . <p> The ocean 's most iconic predators maintain warmer body temperatures than the surrounding seawater and are paying an increasingly steep price for it . As the oceans warm due to climate change , they now face the risk of potentially fatal overheating , according to a new report in Science . <p> Several large tuna species and sharks , known as " mesothermic " species for the way their bodies run hot , require more fuel to maintain their temperature and are thus confronting a " double jeopardy " of warming oceans and declining food , mainly from overfishing . As water temperatures climb , these species will be forced to relocate to cooler waters . <p> " If you 're a shark , you ca n't just pop down to the supermarket and buy more food , " said Nick Payne , lead author and associate professor at Trinity College Dublin , Ireland . " We 're seeing animals move with climate change in every biome on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ example of that mechanism . " <p> From South Africa 's powerful great whites to Ireland 's filter-feeding basking sharks , these mesotherms burn nearly four times as much energy as their cold-blooded counterparts , whose body temperatures match the surrounding water . As oceans warm , these species must slow down , alter their blood flow or dive to cooler temperatures , all while hunting for an ever-dwindling food supply . <p> A rare group comprising fewer than 0.1 percent of all marine life , mesothermic fishes -- also including thresher and porbeagle sharks -- trap metabolic heat to keep their bodies warmer than surrounding seawater . This has been evolutionarily key to enabling higher swimming speeds , enhanced predation and their long-distance migrations . <p> However , as fish grow larger , their bodies generate heat faster than they can shed it . This mismatch -- driven by the physics of surface area and heat retention -- triggers the overheating dilemma in warmer waters . <p> While some species like Atlantic bluefin tuna can temporarily boost their heat loss or dive to colder waters , the suitable habitats @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ become inhospitably hot . This will be especially the case during summer months when sharks will experience increased competition for prey . <p> This will disrupt ecosystems as mesotherms are typically apex predators that exert disproportionate control on species below them in the food chain , said Edward Snelling , co-author and physiologist at the University of Pretoria . <p> " These species are being pushed closer to their physiological limits , which could have consequences for where they can live and how they survive , " said Snelling in a press release . " These animals are already operating on a tight energy budget , and climate change is narrowing their options even further . " <p> Using tiny sensors on a range of fish , including basking sharks weighing over three tons , researchers calculated how much heat fish produce and lose in real time . From this , they calculated that a one-ton warm-bodied shark may struggle to remain in waters above 62.6 ? Fahrenheit ( 17 ? Celsius ) without taking countermeasures . Discovering these " hidden heat budgets " could prove critical to any hope @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> In South Africa , the stakes are both ecological and cultural . Here , great whites have emerged as a " sentinel species " : When their patterns change , it signals a deeper shift in the marine ecosystem . <p> While long sensationalized as feared predators , they 've increasingly become icons of marine conservation and eco-tourism , said Stephanie Nicolaides , a marine conservation researcher at the University of the Western Cape . " Many local and international conservation narratives now position the great white not as a villain , but as a keystone species essential to maintaining ocean health , " Nicolaides said . <p> Declines of great white sightings in False Bay , Mossel Bay , and Gansbaai , however , are multifaceted . Though thermal relocation may be a contributor , their population decline is also linked to a history of overfishing , shark netting , and habitat destruction . <p> Indeed , though warming waters heighten mesotherms ' vulnerability worldwide , other manmade harms exert the most danger . " If we had to say what is the one thing that we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the fishing problem , " said Payne . " The most acute , urgent crisis these animals face is from overfishing , and particularly now from bycatch . " <p> Bycatch refers to fish and other marine animals caught unintentionally by fishermen using huge nets or long lines baited with thousands of hooks . <p> History , however , offers a grim precedent for physiological vulnerability itself . Fossils of extinct warm-bodied species -- like the infamous Megalodon shark , which reached almost 60 feet long -- suggest they suffered disproportionately during past ocean temperature increases as they likely struggled to secure food to fuel their large , warm bodies . <p> Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years . With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences , Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information . After all , you do n't need to know everything , only what 's important .

@@136425680 <p> AWENDAW -- The spring morning is unseasonable cool when the crew drops a line of flames along the edge of a pine forest . <p> As the flames smolder slowly across the forest floor , the team closely monitors the beginning of the prescribed fire before Chelsea MacKenzie , the " burn boss " gives the go-ahead to continue . <p> Fire is a notoriously uncontrollable element , but under the right conditions , controlled flames can help restore and maintain South Carolina 's forests , and avoid catastrophic wildfires that might otherwise burn . <p> The Post and Courier 's Rising Waters Lab focuses on impacts of climate change and related policies and practices . It is supported by donations and grants to the nonprofit Public Service and Investigative Fund , whose contributors are subject to the same coverage we apply to everyone else . For more information and to make a donation , go to **25;357;TOOLONG sign up for our newsletter , click here . <p> It 's a practice older than the state itself . The area 's indigenous people would conduct burns to manage the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ colonizers who favored fire suppression . No fire was set intentionally , and any flame detected was snuffed out as quickly as possible . <p> Today , forest managers and scientists understand the benefits of prescribed burns , and spring days often mean a haze of smoke hanging over parts of the Lowcountry . The burns help clear undergrowth , allowing wildlife to move through the forest and encouraging the development of grasses and other plants that make up a diverse and healthy understory . There is always a need for these intentionally set fires in the woods , but fragmented forest land , encroaching development and climate change all make the practice more difficult . <h> How to burn <p> Before the first twig is set aflame , the six-member crew gathers in front of the property 's cabin at The Nature Conservancy 's Driftwood Preserve in Awendaw . MacKenzie talks the group through the day 's plan . The operation is laid out in a five-page packet designating everything from property lines to crew positions , as well as the best place to land a helicopter should someone @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fire at Driftwood Plantation in Awendaw . The team uses the burn to reduce wildfire risk , aid conservation for longleaf and loblolly pine , and help the forest 's ecosystem . <p> Some of the crew working on the Driftwood Preserve are Nature Conservancy staff based in the Southeast , save for Josie Meyer , who 's position has her moving between South Carolina and Minnesota . Two members of the team are wildland firefighters who spend their summers fighting wildfires in the west . <p> Out west , firefighters mostly suppress flames to avoid massive blazes that make headlines every summer . The crews that come together to do prescribed burns in South Carolina tend to be smaller teams , which allows the members to be more independent , says Patrick Ma , a conservation forester with The Nature Conservancy . The setup allows for members of the crew to complete different training certifications in the state . <p> " We hope that a little bit of South Carolina travels back with them to their home units , " Ma says . <p> The team drives out to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ plot , with everyone close by . Quickly , grey smoke fills the air as the burn gets underway . Crew members with the drip torches burn along the edge of a designated area , and the flames move into the interior of the forest , followed by more fire placed by the crew . Another team walks up and down the edge of the burn to ensure no flames escape the designated burn area . It 's a job that lends itself to long days moving around flames and through copious amounts of smoke . <p> If the smoke bothers them , no one shows it . The crew is precise and efficient . Their communication is exact , delivered quickly over radios and employing acronyms and phrases unique to the industry . <p> Kyle Keeley , with The Nature Conservancy , walks along the disc line before heading back into the interior of a prescribed burn operation at Driftwood Plantation , Wednesday , March 18 , 2026 , in Awendaw . <p> Gavin McIntyre/Staff <p> Other than " burn boss " MacKenzie , each team member has a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " They start by creating a " black line , " which should not go into the " green . " The " disc lines " are barriers of bare soil that serve as firebreaks , freshly dug by a farmer who lives on the property . If fire has a language , the crew has learned to speak it . <p> Managed burns require specific conditions in order for the operation to proceed safely . In South Carolina , prescribed fires typically are done in the spring , when weather is more predictable , Ma says . Things like relative humidity , wind direction and drought conditions matter when planning a burn , so the team gets organized a day or two in advance . <h> Less elbow room <p> Historically , the biggest concern has been keeping the flames within the fire break . Today , crews also strive to keep the smoke from blowing into nearby neighborhoods , more and more of which are popping up near forestland , says Jonathan Calore , assistant law enforcement chief with the South Carolina Forestry Commission . <p> In the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ found the midst of forests , and that can impede crews from conducting controlled burns required to keep the forests healthy -- and less likely to burn in dangerous ways . So wind direction is a factor that has become increasingly important , Ma says . <p> Climate change is n't helping the issue either . A 2024 study published in Nature found that more variable weather patterns driven by climate change means fewer days with suitable conditions for prescribed fire in the eastern United States . <p> In 2025 , the spring had been too wet to organize burns , until recently . The forest might be wet on the surface , but that does n't mean there are n't underlying drought conditions , Calore says . <h> A better forest <p> Longleaf pine once covered over 90 million acres of the Southeast . The species evolved with fire , and in it 's absence much of the historic range has given way to the loblolly pine . The more competitive loblolly species also benefits from prescribed burns , but longleaf is much more tolerant of flames in its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have taken root at Driftwood Preserve . <p> Brush and other foliage is overtaken by flames during a prescribed burn operation at Driftwood Plantation , Wednesday , March 18 , 2026 , in Awendaw . <p> Gavin McIntyre/Staff <p> Longleaf forests are the ideal home for the red-cockaded woodpecker , a federally threatened species . Because longleaf pine oozes sap whenever something creates a hole in its bark , the red-cockaded woodpecker will create small holes around its nest , Ma said . The sap creates a sticky barrier against rat snakes that would like to eat the bird 's eggs and chicks . <p> Without prescribed fire , hardwood trees would grow quickly and create a ladder for these snakes to easily reach the nest , Ma says . Loblolly does n't produce the same amount of sap , making the tree a less than ideal home for the bird . <p> The hardwood trees that would pop up in the absence of fire would also crowd out and shade the understory grasses and plants that many animals , such as turkey , rely on for habitat . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the loblolly pine on Driftwood Preserve , Ma says . Longleaf was restored on a portion of the property after a section of loblolly was clearcut years ago . <p> With the decline of the timber market and the closing of local paper mills , the trees are staying in place , creating a more crowded forest , and increasing the need for prescribed burns , Ma says . Hopefully , the longleaf seeds can take advantage of that .

( Photo by Sophia Germer , The Times-Picayune ) <p> STAFF PHOTO BY SOPHIA GERMER <p> Three days before flying to Italy for the most important exhibition of her life , New Orleans artist Dawn DeDeaux was striding to and from her kitchen , forks in one hand , spoons in the other . <p> She paused beside her friend Susan Taylor , director of the New Orleans Museum of Art , for a moment to chat , cheerily , about the end of the world . <p> " A black hole in the sea ! It will self-subsume , " DeDeaux said , setting the utensils on a 15-foot dining table that spans the living room of her Gentilly home , an old po-boy shop that she has remade -- artwork by artwork , object by object -- into an exhibition of its own kind . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ her arms into the air before wrapping them around a guest who had arrived to her dinner party . " Look who it is ! " <p> For decades , the 73-year-old conceptual artist has been creating art that explores the universe 's most pressing questions with photography and video , sculpture and installation . Behind each work is an academic study that struck her , a nonfiction book that moved her , a philosophy that tweaked her previous beliefs . <p> Oftentimes , all three . " I ca n't look at anything in a singular way , " she said . " It 's all relational . " <p> But despite their grand scale and heady inspiration , DeDeaux 's works are intimate , human . She builds a world -- then invites you in . <p> Dawn DeDeaux greets Filippo Meozzi and Paolo Meozzi at the door during a dinner party at Camp Abundance in New Orleans , Wednesday , April 1 , 2026 . ( Photo by Sophia Germer , The Times-Picayune ) <p> STAFF PHOTO BY SOPHIA GERMER <p> " If you give her a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will inevitably be transformed into something marvelous , " said New York-based curator Dan Cameron , who did just that for Prospect New Orleans , the contemporary art triennial , among other exhibitions . <p> But she 's not creating environments only for art exhibitions . Her home , a cluster of four buildings she 's dubbed Camp Abundance , " pushes the bounds of what art can be , " Cameron said . It feels both antique and futuristic , old-world and space-age . White columns , inscribed with the epic poem " Paradise Lost , " stand near the camp 's entrance . Clear acrylic ladders , offering a fragile escape from this world , lean against wood slats . Massive metal plates , embossed with soft images of swamps and trees , cover one wall . <p> Being picked for the career-making Biennale , arguably the contemporary art world 's most important show , is an honor DeDeaux never anticipated . <p> It 's a chance for the world to discover what New Orleans knows and New York City ought to have learned by now , Cameron @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ get the recognition she deserves . " <p> Camp Abundance , artist Dawn DeDeaux 's home base , " pushes the bounds of what art can be , " a curator said . <p> STAFF PHOTO BY SOPHIA GERMER <h> ' A front-row seat to the future ' <p> For years , DeDeaux wondered if she 'd made a mistake by remaining in New Orleans . <p> She 'd grown up here , the eldest of six children . When two siblings died in the same year , her family ruptured and her empathy grew . DeDeaux moved into her grandmother 's house on Esplanade Avenue , a short walk from NOMA , where she witnessed White flight house by house , block by block . <p> A bohemian artist arrived , renting a room upstairs . She taught DeDeaux to paint , introduced her to literature and brought her to New York City galleries . <p> Taking courses at one college , then the next , DeDeaux met Robert Yarber , a painter and her " first great love . " When another sibling got sick , spurring her @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his work and so unaccustomed to loss that he did n't support her the way she needed , she said . She broke it off . <p> But Yarber , who went on to exhibit in the 1984 Venice Biennale , would reappear in her story , decades later . <p> DeDeaux began making more conceptual work -- first about racial divides , then about the environment , always about justice . <p> Celebrated multimedia artist Dawn DeDeaux , whose work finds beauty in calamity , poses in her studio at Camp Abundance in New Orleans in March . ( Photo by Sophia Germer , The Times-Picayune ) <p> STAFF PHOTO BY SOPHIA GERMER <p> Pieces of those works now stand among her backyard ginger plants , lean against the fence , and hang from the walls of Camp Abundance , which she 's been restoring with the help of former neighbor and longtime collaborator Gary Craddock for more than a decade . <p> In the 1970s , she hooked up telephone booths to CB radio channels , encouraging conversation across New Orleans neighborhoods , races and divides . ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2021 retrospective at NOMA , stands in her garden . ) In the 1990s , her stately photographs of young Black men were shown as part of a controversial show at the Whitney Museum -- and subpoenaed by the FBI . ( They fill boxes atop a closet in a cottage she offers up for artist residencies . ) <p> " I think Dawn is fundamentally inclined to want to see the wrongs of the world righted , somehow , " Cameron said , tackling issues of " race , gender , class -- topics you 're not supposed to talk about at dinner parties . " <p> She was already exploring themes of escape and extinction when Hurricane Katrina hit in 2005 . <p> She got out " in a van loaded with her computers , three neighbors who were afraid to go to the Superdome , an out-of-towner who had been staying with her and working on her website , six finches , an aged dog and a cat , " as a 2014 New York Times article put it . <p> Returning a month later , she @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a third of her work destroyed . Then , the following year , when she was out of town , her studio burned down . <p> And yet . She believes the hurricane and its aftermath flipped New Orleans ' vantage point , freeing it from its own nostalgia and romanticized history . " Salt water is eating away at the marsh grass that holds that mud together . Undercurrents are pulling at it . " So we are the youngest land mass and the first to go . And with increasingly unpredictable geopolitics posing a threat to the continued supply of fuel , how will the industry power its planes ? <p> Recent events have seen the cost of oil go over $100 a barrel which has led to jet fuel doubling in price , jumping from $750 to $1,500 . <p> In this episode of The Big Question , CEO of EcoCeres , Matti Lievonen joined Angela Barnes in the studio to discuss why their sustainable aviation fuel ( SAF ) could provide the solution . <h> How is sustainable aviation fuel made ? <p> EcoCeres @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ traditional jet fuel , but instead of mining for fossil fuels , they use existing feedstocks like used cooking oil , animal fat and fish fat . <p> The benefits ? Up to 90% less greenhouse gas emissions . <p> The Hong Kong based company already has partnerships with 350,000 restaurants , ranging from McDonalds and Subway across China , who provide their used cooking oil for use in EcoCeres ' production process . <p> Having developed their own technology to filter and process these " dirty feedstocks , " Matti proudly declared that EcoCeres ' input to output conversion is " the best in the world , " boasting an 85% yield . <p> Though EcoCeres is a relatively new company , still considering themselves a start-up , they 're already the second biggest producer in their field , with an annual output of around 770,000 tonnes per year . <p> And with eyes on plans to build a third and fourth production plant , and looking at the potential to start production in Europe , that is set to grow . <p> CEO of EcoCeres , Matti Lievonen @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of The Big Question.Euronews <h> Does sustainable aviation fuel cost travellers more ? <p> EcoCeres has already partnered with the likes of British Airways , Lufthansa , KLM and AirFrance . EU regulations currently mandate a 2% SAF mix , rising to 6% in 2030 and again to 70% by 2050 . That said , many airlines are already opting to use a higher mix of SAF before the mandated deadlines . <p> A standard flight between Hong Kong and London needs around 60 tonnes of jet fuel , meaning EcoCeres ' 770,000-tonne output can power tens of thousands of these flights per year . <p> But is there an added cost for travellers ? Matti estimates on the Hong Kong to London route , passengers are looking at a roughly ? 42 premium when using a 10% SAF mix . <p> " I think that we should all , as individuals , take responsibility for SAF because if I ask airlines ' What is your biggest risk ? ' They all say that it 's climate change . It 's not today , but when there is more hazardous @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> " So I think that it 's a common goal that we should decarbonise aviation but also other transports . So it 's really the climate change question but also it 's the security of supply . Now we see that . " <p> Though SAF is currently more expensive to produce , it is protected from the fluctuations in price we see in traditional oil and gas connected to geopolitical developments . Where we have seen recently doubling in price of jet fuel , SAF has only increased 30% . This means the price gap is falling . Before recent crises , SAF was 200-300% of the price of traditional jet fuel , which has now fallen to 150%.

@@136451784 <h> Japan announces new name for days over 40C after hottest summer ever <p> A woman holds a portable fan in Tokyo , Monday , July 23 , 2018 , as searing hot temperatures are forecast for wide swaths of Japan and South Korea in a long-running heat wave . - Copyright AP Photo/Koji Sasahara , File <h> ' Kokushobi ' will be used in weather forecasts to warn that extreme heat is on the way . <p> Japan now has a special name for days that are 40C or hotter . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> ' Kokushobi ' translates as cruelly hot , brutally hot or severely hot . The name won a public vote , with ' chomoshobi ' , meaning super extremely hot day , coming in second place . <p> It is the first time the Japan Meteorological Agency ( JMA ) has been forced to introduce a new term for weather forecasts since 2007 , when ' moshobi ' ( extremely hot day ) was introduced for days over 35C . <h> Japan 's hottest summer ever <p> Summer 2025 was Japan 's hottest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ temperatures 2.36C above average . <p> Temperatures topped 40C on nine days between June and August , with the mercury hitting 41.8C in the city of Isesaki on 5 August - the highest on record nationwide . <p> For comparison , 2024 saw a total of four days of 40C heat , with the highest temperature of 41C recorded in the city of Sano . <p> The JMA has already predicted a hotter-than-average summer this year , with high temperatures expected across the country . <p> Scientists attribute much of this to the climate crisis , particularly the warming of waters around the Japanese archipelago which locks the country into higher temperatures well into autumn . Warmer oceans also fuel heavier rainfall and more intense typhoons . <h> Kokushobi joins list of Japanese names for hot days <p> In an online survey conducted earlier this year , 203,000 people chose ' kokushobi ' as their favourite word for days over 40C . This was more than triple the votes for ' chomoshobi ' , meaning super extremely hot day , which came in second place . <p> Alongside the survey @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that the name is socially familiar and appropriate in the Japanese language , according to the Japan Times . <p> ' Kokushobi ' uses the Japanese character koku ( ? ) which means harsh or cruel . <p> According to news outlet Asahi Shimbun , other suggestions included ' gekiatsubi ' meaning a brutally intense , explosive kind of heat ; ' shakunetsubi ' , evoking a blistering heat that seems to burn the skin ; and ' futtobi ' , a day so hot it feels as though the world itself is boiling . <p> Currently , the JMA categorises days with temperatures reaching 25C or higher as ' natsubi ' meaning summer day . ' Manatsubi ' , meaning midsummer day , for days over 30C , and ' moshobi ' , meaning extremely hot day , for days over 35C . <p> Extreme heat causes a range of health risks , from sunburn and heat stroke to death . It also affects the economy as outdoor industries are unable to operate and schools often have to shut . <p> The past three years - 2024 , 2023 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> A Copernicus report emphasised that there are two main reasons why the years 2023 to 2025 were exceptionally warm . The first is the build-up of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere , from continued emissions and reduced uptake of carbon dioxide by natural sinks like forests . <p> Second is sea-surface temperatures reaching exceptionally high levels across the ocean , associated with an El Ni ? o event and other ocean variability factors , amplified by climate change . <p> While fossil fuels continue to be burned at alarming rates , temperatures will continue to rise , putting millions of lives , homes and businesses at risk . <p> During the current energy crisis , caused by the effective closure of the Hormuz Strait , renewable energy sources like wind and solar have continued to prove their worth . <p> Governments are key to the clean energy transition , by doing things like cutting subsidies for fossil fuel companies . But citizens can also play their part by installing solar at home and reducing their energy consumption .

@@136483685 <h> Gaskin : Earth Day in a " drill baby , drill " world <h> Share this : <p> Volunteers , state workers and members of GreenRoots work to clean it up a salt marsh in celebration of earth day in Chelsea last year . The event was organized by GreenRoots and aims at cleaning up the city . The marsh is part of the Mystic River watershed and connects to Boston Harbor . ( Photo by Joseph Prezioso / AFP ) ( Photo by JOSEPH PREZIOSO/AFP via Getty Images ) <p> Earth Day , first observed in 1970 , was established as a global call to action for environmental protection . It symbolizes humanity 's collective responsibility toward the planet , emphasizing sustainable practices and stewardship . Yet , today , Earth Day exists in tension with a " drill baby drill " mindset -- a phrase epitomizing an aggressive push for fossil fuel exploration and extraction . <p> Historically , Christians have held varied views on environmentalism , evident since the first Arbor Day and Earth Day celebrations . Some Christians embrace these celebrations , aligning them with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Others interpret Genesis 1:26 , where God grants humanity " dominion " over the earth , as divine permission to exploit natural resources extensively . Perhaps these passages together call for a balanced view : humans have been granted authority over the earth , coupled with an obligation to care for it responsibly . <p> The differing views on Earth Day reflect a different understanding of discipleship -- individual , e.g. , I read my Bible , I pray , I go to church , I tithe , and the second group , I engage in community and collective advocacy . The first group may do none or all of the following : recycle , buy energy-efficient appliances , and perhaps drive an electric vehicle , and consider themselves practicing good environmental stewardship . The second group practices sustainable consumption and champions environmental and energy reform as a means of achieving environmental justice , addressing structural inequities that disproportionately harm marginalized communities , particularly Black and brown populations . <p> Christians often disagree about climate change . <p> According to the Pew Research Center , " Evangelical Protestants tend to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to express skeptical views " about climate change . About a third of evangelical Protestants say the Earth is getting warmer mostly due to human activity ( 32% ) , according to Pew , while similar shares say the Earth is getting warmer mostly due to natural patterns ( 36% ) or that they are unsure or not convinced the Earth is getting warmer ( 32% ) . Meanwhile , members of non-Christian religions and people who do not identify with any religion -- particularly self-described atheists and agnostics -- consistently express the highest levels of concern about climate change . Other Christian subgroups , such as Catholics , generally fall somewhere in between . <p> In February 2006 , a group of 86 notable U.S. evangelical Christian leaders launched the Evangelical Climate Initiative , a campaign for environmental reform . Then , in March 2008 , a group of Southern Baptist leaders issued a declaration conceding that the denomination had been " too timid " with respect to environmental issues and acknowledging a need to " take responsibility for our contributions to climate change . " <p> Environmental justice @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ treatment for all races , incomes , and cultures regarding environmental laws and policies . It insists that everyone has a right to equal environmental protections and participation in policy-making . <p> Copyright 2026 Boston Herald . All rights reserved . @@136484282 <h> Opinion : Cuts to agri-food research bad for Canada and your grocery bill <p> The federal government should reverse course on planned closings of seven research centres and farms across the country . <p> A canola field in Alberta at sunset . While the closing of rural research stations might not seem relevant to those living in big cities , all Canadians stand to be affected , write Beatrice Olivastri , John Bennett and Frances Grace Fyfe of Friends of the Earth . ( Mike Drew / Postmedia ) <p> These centres are vital to Canada 's food system . Losing them would also jeopardize our long-term food security and food sovereignty just as climate change impacts intensify . <p> We at Friends of the Earth are adding our voices to those who see this move as short-sighted and are calling on the federal government to reverse course . <p> The Quebec Research and Development Centre in Quebec City is among the research farms and centres set to close within 12 months . The others are in Alberta , Saskatchewan , Manitoba , Ontario and Nova Scotia . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to those living in big cities , all Canadians stand to be affected by the closures of research centres responsible for , among other things , developing organic oats for breakfast and organic wheat for bread and pastry . <p> The closings are expected to lead to the loss of more than 600 highly skilled researchers , technicians and support staff with experience and knowledge in plant breeding , soil health and organic and regenerative crop and livestock research . Decades of public investment in research stand to be discarded . <p> By shuttering these facilities , the AAFC by its own account expects to save $154.7 million in operational spending by 2028-29 . <p> Our calculations suggest these cuts will save the average Canadian taxpayer roughly 52 cents per month . While that might sound fiscally responsible , it is really a false economy -- a short-term move that can be expected to cost Canadians far more for groceries in a future beset with climate , biodiversity and pollution challenges . <p> What is most alarming is that these cuts specifically target organic and regenerative research . This matters @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's a strategy for coping with climate change . <p> Research conducted at these stations helps farmers develop crop varieties that require less synthetic fertilizer -- a substance that is expensive , fossil-fuel-intensive to produce and harmful to the environment . <p> When these stations close , we drastically undercut our ability to test new plant varieties to ensure they can succeed in Canadian conditions , leading to food systems less able to adapt to the droughts and pests that are already driving up food prices . <p> Canada 's organic market is massive , valued at over $10.55 billion in 2023 , with growing consumer demand . Yet domestic organic production has not kept pace , forcing us to import more and lose out on local innovation . <p> Without public research to help farmers transition to organic practices -- practices that are often more intensive to research and implement -- the cost of organic products grown in Canada will likely stay high or we will continue to rely on imports . <p> AAFC research centres also help protect public plant breeding -- supporting publicly funded institutions like universities @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not reliant on large international corporations to tell us how to grow our food . <p> In a time of great change in climate and world politics , we need to reverse the closures and invest in our agricultural research stations to protect economic sovereignty and growth . <p> Beatrice Olivastri is the chief executive officer , and John Bennett is the senior policy adviser , of Friends of the Earth Canada.Frances Grace Fyfe is a Friends of the Earth board member and doctoral student at McGill University .
