@@136701692 <h> Emperor penguins survived everything -- until us <h> In Antarctica , they are running out of ice and time . <p> Susan Rigetti is author of three books and a former editor at The New York Times and Slate.She is a contributing Globe Opinion writer . <p> Emperor penguins have existed in Antarctica for more than 40 million years . Their ancestors appeared on the planet an estimated 60 million years ago -- not long after dinosaurs became extinct -- and they have somehow persisted as a species for all this time , outliving countless other species of birds , mammals , and reptiles that we know only from museum collections . Emperor penguins have survived multiple ice ages , drastic climate fluctuations , and catastrophic extinction events , but they may not survive us . <p> Earlier this month , the IUCN Red List added emperor penguins to the list of endangered species . Over the past decade and a half , scientists have seen a devastating population decline across the approximately 60 colonies of emperor penguins scattered across the vast ice sheets of Antarctica . The population @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sheets , which are melting at an alarming rate , disrupting the life cycle of the penguins and threatening both their near-term and long-term survival . <p> Emperor penguins are completely dependent on the continent 's ice sheets , where they molt , breed , lay and incubate their eggs , and raise their chicks , as immortalized in the documentary " The March of the Penguins . " <p> Each part of their life cycle is timed to seasonal annual changes in the ice . If the ice melts too fast , or at the wrong time , or during one of the vulnerable periods of a penguin 's life ( such as when chicks are too young to swim , or when adult penguins are molting and lack their waterproof feathers ) , the results are deadly . Four years ago , four colonies of emperor penguins lost all their chicks when the sea ice melted too early and the chicks drowned in the icy water . <p> Antarctica is arguably the most protected place on the planet , thanks to the Antarctic Treaty , which prohibits practically @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with a National Geographic expedition several years ago , I was impressed by the strictness and effectiveness of the treaty 's regulations , and rendered speechless by the vast , empty , untouched landscapes . This is what the world was like before humans , I remember thinking . This is what the world would be like without us . <p> It was a nice thought , but I was wrong . Even with the Antarctic Treaty , our southernmost continent is not safe , nor is it untouched . According to NASA , the ice sheets in Antarctica are shrinking by 135billion tons every year due to human-caused climate change . These melting ice sheets are responsible for the sharp decline in emperor penguin populations and also for the disappearance of more than half of the world 's Antarctic fur seals , which were also declared endangered alongside the penguins.If even Antarctica -- the most protected , untouched region of our planet -- is this affected by human abuse and neglect of the rest of Earth , what then ? <p> In his 2016 book " Half Earth : @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ biologist E.O . Wilson proposed that humanity set aside half of Earth ( both land and sea ) for animals and plants in an effort to preserve ( and someday restore ) the planet 's biodiversity . When I first read the book , I thought that the difficult part of his proposal would be carving out the half that would be given to the other creatures of Earth . But now I realize that even if we were to make Wilson 's dream a reality , the most difficult part would be finding a way to prevent humans from indirectly destroying the other half due to our neglect , abuse , and exploitation of our own corners of the world . <p> We need more . The steps that we have taken to protect Antarctica are not enough . It 's not enough to prevent humans from exploiting Antarctica ; activities that harm -- whether directly or indirectly -- the continent need to be restricted and prevented , too . If we do n't radically change course on climate change , the largest species of penguins will soon be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of penguins will follow . And once they are gone , we will never get them back . There will not be any more penguins , nor a new species of the penguin family , because we will have made the world uninhabitable for them . <p> For the past 40 million years , emperor penguins have lived and died and bred and raised their chicks on the impossibly cold ice sheets of Antarctica and swum and dived and fed in the frigid , krill-rich Southern Ocean . They are now endangered , and if we do nothing to stop the human-caused destruction of the sea ice they depend on , they will most likely be extinct by the end of this century . 
@@136705396 <p> Portia Elan says a colleague 's remark about John Steinbeck 's " Cannery Row " set her on the path to writing her debut novel , " Homebound . " <p> Elan , then a brand-new public school teacher , recalls her fellow first-year instructor Brenda Rivera comparing the community in Steinbeck 's novel to the ecosystem of a tide pool . And something clicked . <p> " She said , ' All the characters are holding their own little corner of the tide pool -- fulfilling their own roles within the ecosystem ' , " Elan recalls . " I 've thought about that probably at least four or five times a week since she said that to me almost a decade ago . " <p> The idea of individual impacts driving ecosystem change fuels much of Elan 's " Homebound , " out May 5 from Scribner . In it , Elan writes through the eyes of multiple characters across time and space to explore connection , identity , found family and what a future on this planet might hold for us . <p> There 's Becks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 1983 , all the way to Yesiko , a mercenary captain steering her ship laden with smuggled goods across a flooded Earth in the year 2586 . In between , we see letters from Tamar , a biologist and inventor struggling with the ethically questionable decisions of her technocratic boss , and the inner thoughts of Chaya , a sentient automaton . <p> The connection between these characters and timelines begins with a text-based computer game called " Homebound . " Created by Becks 's beloved uncle , it 's completed by Becks as she mourns his passing , grapples with her queer identity and navigates conflict in her relationships with her mother and best friend . <p> In the game , an astronaut on a deep-space mission to free a generation ship must make a series of choices in order to save the lives of everyone on board . As the timeline skips back and forth , from character to character , Elan shows us the power of storytelling in surviving through eons of change and shaping lives lived hundreds of years from now . <p> Q. You @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ you find your way to writing futuristic science fiction ? <p> My MFA is in poetry , and that 's mostly what I wrote through my mid-20s . Then I actually stopped writing altogether while I worked as a high school teacher , because that 's a job that requires so much of one 's heart and mind . When I became a public librarian , I found I had time and space to write again -- but at that point , I was n't reading a lot of poetry . I was reading a lot of novels . <p> I 'm a pretty omnivorous reader , but I think the books that most sparked that excitement to sit down and write my own stories were " Cloud Atlas " by David Mitchell , " How High We Go in the Dark " by Sequoia Nagamatsu , and the interwoven fairy tales in Emma Donoghue 's " Kissing the Witch . " I was also inspired by " People of the Book " by Geraldine Brooks -- the kind of books that leap from time to time , where , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ little bit of work to understand the connections between the gaps . <p> Q. The book is written from different characters ' perspectives . Whose point of view did you think of first ? <p> I was part of some online writing groups during the pandemic . In one of them , we had a prompt to write a character 's pet peeves . I almost immediately found the voice of this spiky , annoyed teenager , with a vulnerability under her prickliness . I was interested in that vulnerability , so I started following her voice , and in the book , she became Becks . <p> Becks 's big question , and mine for large parts of my life , was , " How do I find where I belong ? " In writing Becks , it became clear to me that the answer to that question was much bigger than any one individual story could hold , because there are actually a lot of answers . So the book was going to require an expansiveness , and the kind of leaping between perspectives that I so enjoy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ you draw on in coming up with the flooded Earth of the future in " Homebound ? " <p> While I definitely exaggerated what 's possible in terms of sea level rise , I did do research into some of the consequences of climate change . That 's where I came upon the term " ecovillages . " They 're intentional communities that exist on every continent , and their focus is on ways of living that are in harmony with their ecosystems and natural environments . Their farming practices , harvesting practices and building practices do n't detract from the health of the land and the animals around them . <p> I spent quite a bit of time reading about those communities , some of them even here in California near where I live . I learned about closed-loop systems like aquaponics , because I was interested in portraying them in the future setting of " Homebound . I played some computer games growing up : The Oregon Trail , Where in the World is Carmen Sandiego . I played Super Mario Bros. at a friend 's house , but I 've never actually owned a game console myself . But I really like the idea that a game is a collaborative story -- that the player is a part of telling the story that the game designer sets out to create . <p> Text-based games are quite intimate because they ask a player to do so much imagining . I was interested in that kind of a relationship to story , as opposed to a more graphic interface where you have a joystick or a controller , and you 're a little bit outside of the game . <p> When I was doing research on early text-based games , I was surprised and delighted by the ways that they could become almost authorless , in that the code would be shared @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or change it , and then pass that code along . Games evolved from person to person . They were n't yet being produced by EA or a big production studio -- they were being written by some guy , and then shared to another person , and then shared to another person . There was a kind of communal ownership that was really interesting to me . <p> Q. In the " Homebound " future , some religious traditions have also survived the passage of time . I think we need ways of feeling that we 've inherited from and connected to those who have come before us , and that our stories are a part of something bigger that will continue on after us . The production of meat for human and animal consumption is responsible for 57% of total global food production emissions , while the global dairy sector alone contributes to 4% of global emissions . The livestock industry alone is one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ between 12% to 19% of total human-caused greenhouse gas emissions . <p> Most of the industry 's emissions can be traced back to methane , which cattle and other livestock burp up as part of their digestive process . Methane makes up more than half of meat and dairy 's climate pollution , and traps 86 times more heat than carbon dioxide over a 20 year time frame . <p> Beef , which is 16% more expensive than a year ago , has the highest environmental impact -- making it the perfect candidate to swap out in favor of a plant-based protein source , like tempeh or tofu . " Beef is clearly the one to avoid , " notes Eshel . <p> Advertisement <h> Is being vegetarian better for climate change ? <p> Research has shown that what we eat really can have an environmental impact . One 2023 study found that plant-based diets produce 75% less heat-trapping gas , generate 75% less water pollution , and use 75% less land for growing than meat-rich diets . A well-balanced vegetarian meal should include a mix of fruits and veggies , whole grains , and plant-based proteins like lentils , tofu , or beans . <p> Advertisement <p> What 's more , eating less meat lowers the risk of heart disease and , one study found , could improve life expectancy . Management uses this measure to assess how well the Company 's producing mines are performing and to assess the overall efficiency and effectiveness of the mining operations and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ during the reporting period . <p> All-in Sustaining Costs Per Payable Pound of Copper Produced All-in sustaining costs per payable pound of copper produced is an extension of the C1 cash costs measure discussed above and is also a non-GAAP key performance measure that management uses to monitor performance . Management uses this measure to analyze margins achieved on existing assets while sustaining and maintaining production at current levels . Consolidated All-in sustaining costs includes sustaining capital and corporate general and administrative costs . <p> Attributable Net debt / Net cash Attributable net ( debt ) / net cash is a non-GAAP performance measure used by the Company to assess its financial position and is calculated as net debt / net cash excluding amounts attributable to or guaranteed by non-controlling interests . <p> Available Liquidity Available liquidity is a non-GAAP performance measure used by the Company to assess its financial position and is composed of RCF credit capacity , cash and cash equivalents and short-term investments , being representative of the Company 's access to liquidity that is available for general purposes . <p> Adjusted net income attributable to shareholders @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Net income ( loss ) attributable to shareholders as reported , adjusted for certain types of transactions that in the Company 's judgment are not indicative of normal operating activities or do not necessarily occur on a regular basis . <p> EBITDA EBITDA is a non-GAAP measure of net income ( loss ) before net finance expense , tax expense , and depletion and amortization . <p> Adjusted EBITDA Adjusted EBITDA is non-GAAP measure of EBITDA before the pre-tax effect of the adjustments made to net income ( loss ) ( above ) as well as certain other adjustments required under the RCF agreement in the determination of EBITDA for covenant calculation purposes . <p> The adjustments made to net income ( loss ) and Adjusted EBITDA allow management and readers to analyze the Company 's results more clearly and understand the cash-generating potential of the Company . <p> Sustaining Capital Sustaining capital is expenditures to maintain existing operations and sustain production levels . @@136740480 <h> Share this : <p> Ronan Miller , seven , drinks melted ice while standing in front of a mister to cool off during hot weather before a baseball game between the Baltimore Orioles and the Texas Rangers , Monday , June 23 , 2025 , in Baltimore . ( AP Photo/Stephanie Scarbrough ) <p> Last month , the Maryland Supreme Court ruled that climate change lawsuits filed by local governments , including Baltimore and other jurisdictions , could not move forward on the grounds that federal law overrides state law on air pollution that crosses state lines . The message they sent was clear : Marylanders are responsible for climate impacts , and we wo n't be getting any reprieve any time soon . <p> That debate will continue . But for most Marylanders , the effects of climate change are not playing out in courtrooms . They are showing up in our everyday lives -- and it 's costing families . A recent Johns Hopkins University report found that 73% of residents of Baltimore City and Baltimore County are concerned climate change will harm them personally , with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ This worry is grounded in what communities are seeing around them . Last year , Baltimore reached 105 degrees Fahrenheit during a June heat wave , breaking a record . Severe drought also impacted Baltimore County for many months of the year , leading to water restrictions across the region . And in what may seem as a paradox , Maryland last year saw a record number of flash floods , caused by the impacts of climate change , creating periods of long dry spells followed by sudden torrential rainfall . <p> For households , these impacts are being compounded and are showing up in ways that are hard to ignore . <p> Hotter summers mean higher electricity bills as families rely more on air conditioning just to stay safe . What used to be a few uncomfortable days is now weeks of sustained heat . Similarly , extended dry periods are putting pressure on water systems , increasing costs for households and businesses that rely on consistent access , while utilities may need to invest more to manage limited resources . For families , that translates to higher water @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ becoming more frequent and more costly . Communities across the state are dealing with stronger storms and infrastructure that was not built for these conditions . Local governments are investing in upgrades and repairs , but those costs do not disappear . They show up in higher taxes , rising insurance premiums and increased utility costs that families ultimately have to absorb . <p> And while these impacts are being felt across the state , they are not being felt equally . <p> Communities of color in Maryland are more likely to live in neighborhoods that experience higher temperatures due to less tree cover and more pavement . These heat islands , as they 're called , experience surface temperatures that far exceed outside ambient temperatures . Urban flooding also disproportionately impacts Black neighborhoods , which tend to exist in low-lying areas or in neighborhoods without the green spaces that would normally absorb water . At the same time , many of these households already pay a higher share of their income on energy , leaving less room to absorb rising costs . <p> This is why climate change can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Maryland , it is already an affordability issue , and one that is deepening existing inequities . <p> Last month 's court decision may have decided where and if legal battles take place , but it does not change the reality for Maryland 's families . The costs are already here , they are growing , and we need help . <p> Leaders now have the opportunity to meet the moment . <p> As lawmakers work to address both climate change and rising costs of living , they should keep communities of color at the center of their approach . That means investing in energy efficiency programs that lower bills for households that need it most . It means prioritizing flood mitigation and infrastructure improvements in the communities facing the greatest risks . And it means ensuring that , as utility companies work to make energy systems more climate resilient , they do not leave the most vulnerable families paying more . <p> Maryland has taken important steps on climate . But progress can not only be measured in emissions reduced . It has to be measured in terms of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- can afford to live and remain in their homes . Climate change is here , and so are its impacts . @@136748388 <p> A 4-day workweek could help with burnout : Life KitThe five-day workweek can feel as preordained as the number of minutes in an hour , but it has n't always been the norm . Workers fought for a Monday-to-Friday schedule before it became standard in the U.S. in the 1930s . Since then , the nature of work has changed a lot , and now , workers are once again fighting for better conditions , says U.K.-based researcher Will Stronge . <p> In this episode , Stronge , who co-wrote the book Overtime : Why We Need a Shorter Working Week , discusses what has changed since the ' 30s and what a shorter workweek offers workers and organizations . <p> A lot of things have changed since the five-day workweek began in the 1930s , and what 's now considered the normal workweek does n't support many workers , says U.K.-based researcher Will Stronge . Olivia Fields for NPR hide caption <p> toggle caption <p> Olivia Fields for NPR <p> A lot of things have changed since the five-day workweek began in the 1930s , and what 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , says U.K.-based researcher Will Stronge . <p> Olivia Fields for NPR <p> The five-day workweek can feel as preordained and immovable as the number of minutes in an hour . You wake up , go to work and come home . Then , wash , rinse , repeat until the weekend . <p> But the Monday-to-Friday grind has n't existed forever . Nearly a century ago , working six days a week was the norm . In the U.S. , the five-day workweek ( along with the two-day weekend ) is something workers fought for and won in the 1930s after working years in grueling conditions that are now illegal . <p> The world has changed a lot since the five-day workweek became enshrined into federal law . But we 've kept working 40 hours from Monday through Friday even though that schedule no longer supports many workers . <p> " The normal working week does n't work in many ways , " says U.K.-based researcher Will Stronge , who co-wrote the book Overtime : Why We Need a Shorter Working Week with Kyle Lewis . " It 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do it . " <p> Stronge says being at work for eight hours of the day does n't mean you 're operating at peak productivity for those eight consecutive hours . But that 's what the five-day workweek suggests . Meanwhile , workers in all kinds of industries are suffering from burnout and blurred lines between their professional and personal lives . <p> So , how did we get here , and where can we go ? Life Kit spoke with Stronge about when the five-day workweek became normal , what has changed since then and what a shorter workweek offers workers and organizations . Excerpts from the conversation , edited for length and clarity , are below . <h> Interview highlights <h> How we got to the five-day workweek <p> Coming out of World War I and World War II , workers basically wanted a better deal . You had President Franklin D. Roosevelt in the U.S. who said : Look , we want a welfare system . We want better support for our society . Frances Perkins , Roosevelt 's secretary of labor , was very much informed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . So you get the Fair Labor Standards Act in 1938 baking in the 40-hour working week into legislation to set new standards for what the working week is . <p> Sponsor Message <h> How work has changed since the five-day workweek became normal <p> In the U.S. , we 've gone from large manufacturing economies to more service-based economies . The U.S. still has a lot of manufacturing , of course . But now , there 's a lot more desk-based work , particularly since the IT revolution of the ' 80s and ' 90s . <p> I think what 's happened since is that our working culture has changed to be one where it 's much more about going above and beyond -- working beyond your hours either for better career prospects or simply because it is demanded of you by your boss . <p> Now , during the pandemic , you 're in your living room with your laptop . So it 's hard to switch off this creep which has infiltrated our working lives . I think it 's safe to say it 's to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ off . <h> How a shorter workweek addresses the " second shift " women perform at work and at home <p> The male breadwinner model has been around since the start of industrialism . Women looked after kids , prepared their meals and also nurtured and looked after the male worker after he came home exhausted from the industrial grind . Throughout the 20th century , when women entered the workplace , women got incomes of their own and lived lives outside of the domestic regime . But they also got the " second shift . " You work your job , you go home , you do the second shift of looking after the family and preparing the meal . <p> So , if you 're talking about reducing working hours in general , a four-day workweek will first and foremost benefit those who work the longest hours in total . This is particularly relevant to women who both have their paid employment and their unpaid work at home . Work is a feminist issue as much as anything else . <h> How the five-day workweek hurts the environment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to people 's carbon emissions or carbon footprints . That 's not just because of the kind of work people do in production ( manufacturing and construction being very carbon intensive ) . It 's not just that . It 's also because of things like commuting . If people drive to work , that 's a huge carbon burden . If you 're taking ready-made meals and bottled water , these kinds of quick , easy foods that come with a work-centered lifestyle , they have high carbon footprints as well . If we 're going to fight climate change , a decent way of doing that while also improving people 's working lives is reducing the work of the working week . <p> Sponsor Message <h> What a shorter workweek offers workers and organizations <p> For many organizations , what you lose in labor time you gain in greater productivity on the job . For a lot of desk-based fields -- creative organizations , administrative organizations and small manufacturers as well -- there 's a recognition that in an eight-hour day , there is some slack . We @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 're overworked and burned out . So reducing the working week has reaped dividends in terms of productivity and worker well-being , which means workers come to work refreshed . They come to work liking their job a bit more and wanting to get the work done so that they can have a nice weekend . <h> The biggest challenges to shortening the workweek <p> The main obstacle for implementing a shorter workweek is changing the work culture . You might have people who want to work above and beyond . They might want to prove that they 're working hard by putting in extra hours . But that 's detrimental because a workplace with a decent working culture would be one where the quality of work is good , where everyone 's playing their role and collaborating within the team . It 's not about individually proving that you 're a harder worker than others . So , laying down firm guidelines and ground rules about what working hours are and what 's expected of staff -- that 's what needs to be in place to avoid overwork culture @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> I think we should all be interested in the future of work . We 're all workers of one kind or another , whether it 's manufacturing or service sector or radio . The future is something that we can , and should , shape . And I think we should steer away from this wave of automation coming in , the jobless future , and so on . We should not give in to the temptation to think that the future is just on its way to impose stuff on us . But we should be interested in the future of work so we can change it . <p> Sponsor Message <h> How we 'd fill time on our day off <p> This sounds quite banal , but a lot of people just get their life admin done so the weekends are entirely clear to do all the fun stuff they want to do . I 've met people who say they used to brew beer and they want to get back into that . And a lot of people just want to spend more time with their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on , which I think is quite heartwarming . <p> The audio portion of this episode was produced by Clare Marie Schneider , with engineering support from Josh Newell . <p> We 'd love to hear from you . If you have a good life hack , leave us a voicemail at 202-216-9823 , or email us at LifeKit@npr.org . Your tip could appear in an upcoming episode . 
@@136769188 <h> Climate change is coming for Cabernet . Here 's how one Napa winery is getting ready <p> Charbono is one of many grape varieties Larkmead Vineyards has planted in an experimental research block to get ahead of climate change . <p> Provided by Rob Watermeyer/Larkmead Vineyards <p> In 2019 , the greatest threat to California wine was climate change . It felt like a reckoning was coming , especially to Napa Valley , where the future of its crown jewel grape , Cabernet Sauvignon , was being called into question : In 30 years , will it be too hot to produce Cabernet at an elite level ? <p> " There 's going to come a point with Cabernet in Napa where you have it seared on the outside and completely raw on the inside , " winemaker Dan Petroski told the Chronicle in 2019 . <p> Then , suddenly , winemakers stopped talking so much about climate change . <p> Climate change was largely put on the back burner , and the threat , though still looming , has been easy to ignore . Recent growing seasons have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have n't been many wildfires . <p> San Francisco Chronicle Logo <h> See more S.F. Chronicle on Google <p> That 's not to say climate change has been forgotten entirely . Dozens of growers now bring sheep , and sometimes pigs , into their vineyards to help with fire mitigation . Others are implementing shade cloths and unorthodox trellis systems to shield grapes from direct sun , or misters to lower the temperature by a few degrees . A few wineries are being extra ambitious : At Larkmead Vineyards in Calistoga , winemaker Avery Heelan has spent the past seven years quietly experimenting with grape varieties that are mostly foreign to Napa , but not to high temperatures . Now , Larkmead is debuting the trial 's first wines . <p> According to the Winkler Index , a scale developed at UC Davis that maps which grape varieties can grow within specific temperature bands , Cabernet can grow successfully in Winkler regions II , III and IV . Napa Valley , a cool region II roughly 70 years ago , is now solidly in regions III and IV , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , region V -- a no-fly zone for Cabernet , which originated in Bordeaux , a more moderate climate than Napa ( that is also experiencing the impacts of climate change ) . Some experts have called the index flawed , but between 2006 and 2019 , Petroski , then Larkmead 's head winemaker , tracked a change in Napa 's climate in real time : The winery 's annual Cabernet harvest was starting almost four weeks earlier due to increasingly higher temperatures . <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad <p> If the overall trend continues , grapes could reach sugar ripeness before the flavors fully develop , and the resulting wines will be completely out of balance . So in 2019 , Petroski planted a three-acre research block at the winery 's estate with grape varieties that are known to thrive in warm climates . He planted old-school California grapes that are naturally resilient to heat and drought , such as Charbono , Zinfandel , Petite Sirah and Chenin Blanc , plus Tempranillo from Spain , Touriga Nacional from Portugal , Shiraz from Australia , and Aglianico @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ block , which required a significant financial investment , was not to replace Cabernet , which , like most Napa Valley wineries , is Larkmead 's bread and butter . Instead , he wanted to find grapes that could supplement it -- blend harmoniously with Cabernet and fill in where it might be lacking 30 years from now due to earlier ripening . In hotter temperatures , these grapes can ideally maintain traits such as color , tannin and acidity better than Cabernet or other traditional Bordeaux varieties , such as Merlot and Cabernet Franc , that are used for blending . <p> Petroski left Larkmead in 2020 . Heelan , who trained under him , took over winemaking and the research block , and she 's made a few changes . She took out the Zinfandel , which has already proven to be drought-resistant in California , and replaced it with Italy 's Nero d'Avola . She also removed the Touriga Nacional and planted the Greek white grape Assyrtiko in its place . " There 's a reason Touriga Nacional is made into port , " she said . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ too floral . I did n't love it , and I do n't think it and Cabernet are going to be the best of friends when blending . " <p> In 2023 , Heelan harvested her first fruit from the research block and last spring , Larkmead released its first wine , a Chenin Blanc . " Customers are loving it , " she said -- so much so that she expects to plant more . <p> This fall , Larkmead will release two more research block wines , both unorthodox red blends . The first , named Contour , is a Tempranillo-dominant wine with Cabernet , Aglianico , Malbec and Zinfandel ; Heelan called it a " baby step " from a traditional Cabernet blend . The other , named Keyline , is a true outlaw , though still plush and structured like a Cabernet , featuring Shiraz as its base with Malbec , Petite Sirah , Charbono , Touriga Nacional and Zinfandel . " I do n't think consumers have tasted blends like this before , " Heelan said . <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Larkmead 's research block project is still in its infancy , Heelan said , for the vines are very young and they have n't been tested . She also plans to transition to dry farming in the next few years . " We have n't had a really hot vintage since we 've had fruit , " she said . " Once we have another heat wave , it will be really interesting to see how resilient they are . " <p> There may even be a collateral benefit to the project : Helping Larkmead chip away at some of the industry 's more pressing problems . All of the research wines are priced between $75-$85 , a value compared to Larkmead 's Cabernets , which start at $125 . That price point , the colorful labels , featuring the art of Larkmead proprietor Kate Solari Baker -- a major departure from the winery 's classic look -- and the experimental nature of the research block wines could appeal to younger drinkers , who tend to seek out more affordable , esoteric options . " It 's part of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Being able to offer a red wine from our estate , under $100 , that 's very exciting to me . It 's a step in the right direction . " <p> Jess Lander joined the food and wine team at the San Francisco Chronicle as wine reporter in 2022 . Her writing encompasses the California wine industry -- from Santa Barbara to Mendocino -- with a focus on Napa Valley and Sonoma County . Jess reports on winery and vineyard acquisitions ; controversial Napa land use debates ; wildfires ; a growing farmworker rights movement ; and Wine Country 's most exciting restaurant and tasting room openings . Occasionally , Jess also writes about her favorite food : cheese . <p> Originally from Boston , Jess moved to Napa Valley in 2010 and has extensively covered California wine country for numerous national and international publications . In 2021 , Jess published " The Essential Napa Valley Cookbook , " a project that raised more than $100,000 for Napa Valley restaurant workers impacted by the pandemic. 
@@136769197 <p> Billionaire Tom Steyer aims to succeed where other self-funding politicians before him have failed : using his personal fortune to win the most powerful job in California . <p> He 's already spent more than Republican Meg Whitman , the previous record-holder . So far , Steyer has spent $146 million . <p> He 's differentiating himself from other billionaires like Google founder Sergey Brin who have recently become very active in California politics . Brin and other billionaires have organized to defeat a proposed billionaire tax , and have spent millions to support San Jose Mayor Matt Mahan , one of Steyer 's rivals . But Steyer is running in the opposite direction , on a platform of raising taxes on big companies and billionaires like himself to facilitate more state spending on progressive policies . <p> He 's also trying to distinguish himself from the other five major Democratic candidates in the race by hewing to the liberal side of the crowded field . <p> San Francisco @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ In a recent interview with the Chronicle , Steyer laid out an ambitious agenda .
